AlphaGenome 2: Teaching AI to read the entire human genome

Google DeepMind(942.0K)·39:47·51.1K views·2026-11-11
Hannah FryProfessor Ziga AvsecResearcher, Google DeepMind Adam GayosoResearcher, Google DeepMind
TL;DRGoogle DeepMind's AlphaGenome and AlphaGenome 2 are AI models designed to read the 98% non-coding human genome at single-cell resolution, helping researchers decode how genetic variants drive complex diseases and rare conditions.
Genotype vs. Phenotype: Genotype refers to the 3 billion letters of human DNA (A, C, G, T), while phenotype is defined at the cellular level—specifically how genes change their expression across different cell types. The Non-Coding Mystery: The 2% vs. 98% divide: Only 2% of the...

TL;DR

Google DeepMind's AlphaGenome and AlphaGenome 2 are AI models designed to read the 98% non-coding human genome at single-cell resolution, helping researchers decode how genetic variants drive complex diseases and rare conditions.

The Human Genome and the Genotype-Phenotype Gap

  • Genotype vs. Phenotype: Genotype refers to the 3 billion letters of human DNA (A, C, G, T), while phenotype is defined at the cellular level—specifically how genes change their expression across different cell types.
  • The Non-Coding Mystery: The 2% vs. 98% divide: Only 2% of the genome encodes proteins (the "LEGO building blocks"), while the remaining 98% regulates when and where those proteins are produced across various cell types.
  • Complex vs. Mendelian Diseases:
    • Mendelian diseases involve a variant directly causing a disease (e.g., BRCA1 and certain cancers, though even these are complex).
    • Complex diseases involve a risk profile determined by a combination of genetic background and environmental factors.

Evolution of DeepMind's Genomic Models

  • AlphaFold & AlphaMissense: Protein-level focus: After AlphaFold 2 mapped protein structures, DeepMind developed AlphaMissense to fine-tune protein structure predictions into pathogenicity scores for coding mutations.
  • AlphaGenome (2025): Whole-tissue context: Expanded sequence input from 200,000 to 1 million base pairs and increased resolution from 128 base pairs down to a single base pair to predict non-coding DNA function across whole tissues.
  • AlphaGenome Atlas: Pre-computed variant impacts: Contains 9 billion pre-computed letter changes across the human genome, condensing 10,000 output numbers plus evolutionary conservation into a single AVVAI score (AlphaGenome Variant Impact Score) ranging from 0 to ~80 to rank mutations.
  • AlphaGenome 2: Single-cell resolution: Upgraded to predict cell-type-specific variant effects (such as in macrophages or blood cells) and allows laboratories to plug in their own custom data without retraining the model.

Real-World Applications and Case Studies

  • Rare Disease Prioritization: Pediatric epilepsy case: Researchers at the Broad Institute used the AVVAI score to narrow down a list of suspect variants in a 6-week-old baby diagnosed with rare epilepsy, successfully identifying a splicing variant 4 to 5 years later and validating it experimentally via a cell-line assay.
  • GWAS Bottlenecks: Unpacking linkage disequilibrium: Genome-wide association studies (GWAS) often identify correlated chunks of inherited DNA rather than true causal variants; AlphaGenome helps prioritize causal variants, target genes, and relevant cell types.
  • Tool Limitations: High-precision, low-recall: The models act as research tools rather than clinical diagnostics, functioning effectively to flag high-impact variants while occasionally missing edge cases due to the "bowl of spaghetti" physical compaction of DNA.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • AGI vs. Virtual Cell: "I think we're going to have AGI before we have a virtual cell. That's my prediction. I think you're probably right. Intelligence is less hard than biology, it seems." — Adam Gayoso

Notable Quotes

"We bought the book, but it's quite hard to read. But now with AI, that book is gradually becoming more legible." — Professor Hannah Fry (quoting geneticist Eric Lander)
"Life has had four billion years weaving everything into this unimaginable complexity. And by contrast, our genome [AI models] is only 18 months old." — Professor Hannah Fry

I imagine there could be some biologists who are listening to this and thinking, well, this is all very well, but this is all in silico. You're running all of this computationally within a machine. How well can it possibly map onto what actually happens in a Petri dish or within a body. Yeah, I think biology has all sorts of edge cases and exceptions and so forth, which make it very hard to predict. Our models don't fully simulate what's happening inside the cells, and we'll miss a lot of these edge cases, but we think that they're good enough to make useful predictions and give clues to people and point them in the right direction. So we're still very, very far from simulating biology. And really, I think performing experiments is more important than ever. But we think that the models have an important role to play because of their ability to scale up and make predictions at a large scale with fast iteration cycle to make those new discoveries. So what stage are we at the moment then in terms of what these tools can be used for. Can they reliably identify the cause of a rare genetic condition, or are we getting close to that. I think these tools are, I would say, high precision, low recall tools, meaning that whenever these models predict there's a strong effect, they're usually right. But there's a lot they miss. So it's very good when it says there's something going on here. There's almost definitely something going on there. But there's a lot of false negatives. It MRSA lot of stuff. Exactly right. Donald Trump; France on Fire | 60 Minutes Full Broadcast

60 Minutes(4.3M)·43:39·2026-11-11
Donald TrumpPresident of the United States
TL;DRPresident Donald Trump reflects on a brutal midterm election defeat, foreign policy, and his controversial business ties in a post-election interview, while across the Atlantic, severe economic protests in France threaten to catapult right-wing populist Marine Le Pen to the presidency.
Midterm Repudiation: Republican Election Losses: President Trump acknowledged his party's historic defeat in the midterms—losing the House, Senate, and red-state offices in Texas, Kansas, and South Carolina—attributing the backlash to voter dislike of high gasoline prices, the...

TL;DR

President Donald Trump reflects on a brutal midterm election defeat, foreign policy, and his controversial business ties in a post-election interview, while across the Atlantic, severe economic protests in France threaten to catapult right-wing populist Marine Le Pen to the presidency.

Trump's Post-Midterm Reflection and Policy Stance

  • Midterm Repudiation: Republican Election Losses: President Trump acknowledged his party's historic defeat in the midterms—losing the House, Senate, and red-state offices in Texas, Kansas, and South Carolina—attributing the backlash to voter dislike of high gasoline prices, the war in Iran, and unfavorable views of Israel.
  • Immigration & Deportation Reevaluation: Too Tough Policy: Trump admitted his mass deportation policy "might be too tough" and acknowledged that removing long-term, working immigrants from hotels and landscaping businesses hurt him politically.
  • Bipartisan Outreach: Working with Democrats: Despite past insults (such as calling a new Texas Democratic senator "Clapperico"), Trump expressed openness to working with Democrats on immigration reform and avoiding future impeachments.
  • White House Staff Shakeup: Leadership Changes: Just an hour after the 60 Minutes interview, Chief of Staff Susie Wiles was replaced by deputy chief Steven Miller, the architect of the mass deportation policy.

Foreign Policy: Iran, Ukraine, and Defense

  • The Iran War and Nuclear Program: Decapitation Strikes: Trump defended his decision to authorize decapitation strikes on Iran's nuclear facilities using B-2 bombers, claiming the war is essentially over and that Iran's nuclear material is "buried under thousands of tons of rock."
  • Cost Recovery: Nation Reimbursement: Trump asserted that protected allies, including Israel, will eventually pay back the U.S. for all costs incurred during the Iran war.
  • Defense Leadership: Support for Pete Hegseth: Trump reaffirmed his full confidence in Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, crediting him with winning the conflict in Venezuela in less than an hour.
  • Russia-Ukraine War: Imminent Peace Deal: Trump claimed he inherited the conflict due to hatred between Vladimir Putin and Volodymyr Zelenskyy, expressed confidence that the war will end soon because of his intervention, and noted he recently lifted diesel sanctions on Russia to supply global energy needs.

Wealth, Crypto, and Potential Corruption Allegations

  • Net Worth Speculation: Forbes Estimates: Trump denied that his net worth surged by $3 billion since returning to office, stating he devotes almost no time to his finances and highlighting that he is the only president who does not take a salary.
  • Crypto and Foreign Ties: World Liberty Financial: When questioned about potential conflicts of interest regarding his son managing the crypto firm World Liberty Financial—which features investments from United Arab Emirates-connected entities—Trump dismissed the concerns, comparing it to the real estate business of his sons and arguing that every president faces unavoidable conflicts.

Silicon Valley, AI Superintelligence, and UFOs

  • The AI Race with China: Super Intelligence (SI): Trump described himself as an "AI bull," explaining that the U.S. is locked in a high-stakes race with China, where President Xi Jinping is "all in" on super intelligence.
  • Risk Management: Dismissing Doom: Trump rejected Silicon Valley warnings that AI carries a 10% chance of killing humanity, arguing that the risks are exaggerated and manageable, and warning that falling behind China is not an option.
  • UFO Sightings: Military Pilot Accounts: Trump noted that while he is "not too much of a believer," he has held private meetings with top F-22 Air Force pilots who reported encountering round, non-aerodynamic objects traveling at three times their speed.

Mortality and Unforgiving Views on Political Rivals

  • Views on the Afterlife: Heaven and Hell: As a religious person, Trump stated he hopes to go to heaven and avoid hell, but refused to express forgiveness for his political enemies.
  • Refusing Reconciliation: Hatred of Rivals: When asked if he could forgive figures like James Comey or Nancy Pelosi to enter paradise, Trump bluntly replied: "I probably cannot do that, no, because I do hate some of my enemies, and I always will."

France on Fire: Economic Unrest and the Rise of Marine Le Pen

  • National Upheaval: Widespread Chaos: Weeks of protests in France—sparked originally by high school student demonstrations over dilapidated schools and a lack of toilet paper—have ballooned into nationwide riots over economic inequality, capitalism, and President Emmanuel Macron's leadership.
  • Economic Toll: Billion-Dollar Damages: The unrest has damaged 2,500 businesses, injured over 1,500 police officers, and caused more than $1 billion in property damage, including the burning of the historic Limoges town hall and the ransacking of immigrant-owned bakeries like Jemi Maschila's.
  • Marine Le Pen's Populist Rise: Presidential Frontrunner: Marine Le Pen, long-time leader of the National Rally Party, has seen her popularity surge amidst the chaos, positioning her as the favorite to win next April's presidential election.
  • Immigration and Remigration: Stricter Borders: Le Pen blamed immigration for destabilizing French society, rejected claims of racism or discrimination in France, and proposed a "remigration" policy to force out immigrants who express hatred for French culture and laws.

Notable Quotes

*"I think it might be too tough, yeah. And I think ultimately I think ultimately that hurt me."* — President Donald Trump, on his mass deportation policy.
*"I probably cannot do that, no, because I do hate some of my enemies, and I always will."* — President Donald Trump, when asked if he could forgive political rivals like James Comey and Nancy Pelosi to enter heaven.
*"The extreme left creates chaos. They generate violence against people, against the police, against public property. It's the same phenomenon all over."* — Marine Le Pen, on the forces driving the French protests.
*"If someone comes and says I hate you, I despise your culture, I don't want to respect your laws, well, we say to him, listen, sir, the door is wide open. And we might even, for that matter, force you to leave."* — Marine Le Pen, detailing her immigration stance.

GLP-1s Are a Bigger Business Than AI

Invest Like The Best(116.0K)·84:55·12.8K views·2026-11-11
Patrick O'ShaughnessyHost, Invest Like The Best Zach ReitanoCo-founder and CEO, Ro
TL;DRZach Reitano, co-founder and CEO of Ro, argues that the central flaw in the American healthcare system is that the patient is not the customer.
Where patients pay directly—such as with LASIC or when GLP-1 drug prices dropped from $1,300 to $149 by cutting out PBMs—costs fall, quality improves, and care is transformed. Systemic Flaw: The core dysfunction of U.S. healthcare is that the patient is not the customer;...

TL;DR

Zach Reitano, co-founder and CEO of Ro, argues that the central flaw in the American healthcare system is that the patient is not the customer. Where patients pay directly—such as with LASIC or when GLP-1 drug prices dropped from $1,300 to $149 by cutting out PBMs—costs fall, quality improves, and care is transformed.

Healthcare System Structure & Economic Realities

  • Systemic Flaw: The core dysfunction of U.S. healthcare is that the patient is not the customer; whoever controls the flow of money at the point of purchase dictates the system.
  • Spending Breakdown & GDP Share:
    • Healthcare spending sits at 18% of U.S. GDP.
    • ~30-35% of U.S. healthcare dollars go to hospital systems, 20-25% to doctor's offices, and 9-10% to drugs and pharmaceuticals.
    • The U.S. spends ~50% of its healthcare costs on 5% of people.
  • Acute vs. Chronic Care: The U.S. healthcare system excels at acute care (cancer survival rates, heart attacks, strokes are higher on average than OECD countries), but struggles with the "slow clock" of rising chronic disease.
  • Cash-Pay Precedents: Where cash-pay models operate directly, costs drop and quality rises (e.g., LASIC eye surgery, breast augmentation, and direct-to-consumer GLP-1s).
  • The PBM Monopoly: Pharmacy Benefit Managers (PBMs) add administrative bloat and friction. When GLP-1s went direct-to-consumer, list-to-net pricing intermediaries were eliminated, dropping prices from $1,300 to $149 while drugmakers actually captured more gross profit via massive volume.

Ro’s Business & Product Lineup

  • Growth Metrics: Ro scaled from a $250 million run rate to over a $3 billion run rate with fewer people than in 2021 (dropping from 600+ to 400+ employees while 10xing the business).
  • Four Core Categories:
    • Cardio-metabolic health (fastest-growing, anchored by GLP-1s).
    • Sexual health.
    • Dermatology.
    • Fertility.
  • Infrastructure & Integration: Ro built an EMR from scratch, a CLIA-certificate CAP-accredited lab, pharmacies, provider networks, and custom pharmacy software in a condition-agnostic, vertically integrated platform.
  • Insurance & Direct Access: Ro has helped over 1% of the U.S. population check their insurance for free using AI, and offers multi-month subscriptions saving patients $600 to $1,800 annually.

The GLP-1 Revolution & Human Biology

  • Upstream Impact: GLP-1s treat the root causes of major chronic killers—approved for diabetes, obesity, overweight, heart disease, sleep apnea, liver disease, and kidney disease, with ongoing studies for addiction, nerve degeneration, pain management, and cancer prevention.
  • Rewiring Incentives: The majority of GLP-1 growth is direct-to-consumer cash pay, bypassing traditional employer-sponsored constraints and proving that price transparency and direct access work.
  • Societal Parallels: Just as conquering infant mortality, maternal mortality, and infectious disease sparked the Industrial Revolution, tackling chronic diseases upstream will fundamentally reshape human society.

The 2022 Company Refounding & Operational Playbook

  • The Zombie Phase (December 2021): Despite raising $500 million at a $5 billion valuation in January 2021, Ro grew only 9% that year, burning cash with an $800M preference stack and suffering from a bloated, unfocused culture ("macaroon soft").
  • The Reset (Summer 2022):
    • Replaced ~7 VPs between January and May 2022; cut 20% of the organization in May-June.
    • Co-founder Rob passed the baton; all brands were consolidated under the single "Ro" name.
    • Implemented a strict "Strong Attract, Strong Repel" culture with explicitly defined operational principles and a zero-ambiguity "work hard" ethos. An additional 6-7% opted out.
  • Execution & Focus:
    • Sequential vs. Parallel Building: Leaned into sequential development rather than parallel to avoid context-switching and maximize compounding gains.
    • Daily Alignment: Maintained a daily 8:50 AM leadership steering meeting for two straight years to enforce velocity and accountability.
    • Killing Long-Term Roadmaps: Replaced multi-year roadmaps with short-term precision and long-term vision, treating detailed long-term plans as "false precision."

Marketing, Stigma, and Consumer Psychology

  • Permission Structures: Ro uses high-profile brand ambassadors (Serena Williams, Charles Barkley, Megan Fox, Chrissy Mets) to dismantle stigma and create cultural permission structures for patients seeking care for weight loss, sexual health, and preventative screenings.
  • Stigma as a Signpost: Societal tension and discomfort around preventative medications (like GLP-1s or cholesterol treatments) highlight irrational double standards compared to socially accepted health-negative behaviors like alcohol consumption.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • GLP-1 Adoption: Zach Reitano predicts that 50% plus of people will be on some form of a GLP-1 10 years from now. — Zach Reitano

Notable Quotes

Inside Theranos with ex-Employee Tyler Shultz

Y Combinator(2.4M)·176:16·16.8K views·2026-11-11
Tyler ShultzFormer employee and whistleblower, Theranos Jessica LivingstonHost, The Social Radars Carolynn LevyHost, The Social Radars
TL;DRTyler Shultz details his transition from an enthralled Theranos employee and mentee of Elizabeth Holmes to a primary whistleblower who exposed systemic fraudulent medical testing, battling aggressive legal intimidation by the company before finding personal and professional vindication.
Initial Impression: Recruited out of Stanford after meeting Elizabeth Holmes at his grandfather George Shultz's house; initially viewed her as "the next Steve Jobs." Sold on the transformative vision of running any central laboratory test on a single drop of blood...

TL;DR

Tyler Shultz details his transition from an enthralled Theranos employee and mentee of Elizabeth Holmes to a primary whistleblower who exposed systemic fraudulent medical testing, battling aggressive legal intimidation by the company before finding personal and professional vindication.

Background & Early Experience

  • Initial Impression:
      • Recruited out of Stanford after meeting Elizabeth Holmes at his grandfather George Shultz's house; initially viewed her as "the next Steve Jobs."
      • Sold on the transformative vision of running any central laboratory test on a single drop of blood anywhere in the world.
  • Early Red Flags:
      • Completed a summer internship working on antibody engineering far removed from the core device, noticing extreme workplace siloing, locked doors, and no co-workers who had actually seen the product.
      • A parting warning from a colleague: *"You shouldn't come back here. You should go somewhere else and learn how to do real science."*

The Theranos Internal Culture & Red Flags

  • Extreme Secrecy and Paranoia:
      • The company operated under intense silos, overreaching non-disclosure agreements, and heavy turnover.
      • Employees received virtually zero stock options, relying instead on visas, mortgages, and student debt to stay.
  • The Launch and Lab Reality:
      • Joined full-time on the exact day Theranos launched its retail product at Walgreens in Palo Alto, during which the assay validation manager quit after a screaming match with Sunny Balwani.
      • Discovered that zero tests were validated on the proprietary Theranos platform; instead, patient samples were run on third-party commercial equipment bought from Siemens and Roche, or outsourced entirely.
  • Toxic Leadership:
      • Sunny Balwani managed the lab through bullying, fear, and intimidation, pushing employees to run patient samples despite quality control failures.
      • Elizabeth Holmes cultivated an angelic public persona as a visionary healthcare darling while allowing Sunny to run the internal operations with a "mob boss" mentality.

Whistleblowing & Internal Resistance

  • Escalating Concerns:
      • Uncovered severe failures during assay validations, notably a syphilis test displaying unacceptable 65% sensitivity and over 20% false positives among employees.
      • Reached out anonymously to the New York State Department of Health, confirming that Theranos's audit cheating methods violated federal and state regulations.
  • Internal Pushback:
      • Gently raised technical and ethical concerns to Elizabeth Holmes and a vice president, only to be stonewalled, ignored, and gaslit.
      • Emailed a detailed breakdown of observations and data to Holmes, which was forwarded to Sunny Balwani. Balwani responded with a blistering all-red, all-caps attack calling Tyler arrogant, ignorant, and threatening him.
  • Resignation:
      • Submitted a two-week notice, successfully sneaking out a ream of printed email threads hidden under a sweatshirt on his last day.

The Legal Campaign & Intimidation

  • The Grandfather Dynamic:
      • George Shultz, a Theranos board member with a massive percentage of his net worth tied up in exclusive Theranos stock (including a trust fund for Tyler's future children), was heavily manipulated and initially refused to believe the warnings.
  • Boies Schiller Ambush:
      • Summoned to his grandfather's house under the guise of signing a one-page non-disclosure agreement, Tyler was instead ambushed by lawyers from David Boies's firm bearing a Temporary Restraining Order (TRO), an affidavit demanding the names of other sources, and a court appearance notice.
      • Stalled the legal pressure by demanding legal counsel and eventually secured top-tier specialized legal representation, spending $400,000 to $500,000 in legal fees.
  • Surveillance and Fear:
      • Endured months of intimidation, including being followed by private investigators and experiencing severe psychological stress and isolation.

Vindication & Public Exposure

  • Going on the Record:
      • Connected with Wall Street Journal reporter John Carreyrou, providing background information and the core phrase: *"Fraud is not a trade secret."*
      • Formally went on the record in October 2015 as part of Carreyrou's investigative series, dismantling Theranos's public facade and triggering inspections by CMS and the FDA.
  • Happy Resolution:
      • Despite initial friction, repaired relations with his parents and eventually reconnected with a former college housemate in Boston, leading to marriage and starting a family.

Notable Quotes

*"Elizabeth Holmes was the type of person where as soon as she starts speaking, the rest of the room would just go silent. Like she had this way of just locking in an audience. And when she spoke to you, she made you feel like you were the most important person in her entire world in that moment."* — Tyler Shultz
*"When management knows they're being watched, they know that they need that they cannot give the result of a Theranos test because the regulator will know that the answer is wrong... but if it was an unsuspecting patient who walked into Walgreens, they were perfectly comfortable knowingly giving them incorrect medical information."* — Tyler Shultz
*"Why on earth would I want that [trust fund] to go to zero? Like I want that to be worth something... I don't want it to be an excuse for aiding and abetting a fraud... I have no motivation to bring this company down. I would be so happy to be wrong."* — Tyler Shultz
*"Fraud is not a trade secret."* — Tyler Shultz

The Department of Justice just released a new one, actually. Oh. There's one with the IRS. There's another one called the False Claims Act. And I specifically want to highlight the SEC because with their program, the whistleblower is able to recover 10 to 30% of what the SEC recovers if there's a settlement in the lawsuit. Nice. What I think is absolutely amazing about that is that yes, it's great that whistleblowers can get paid for blowing the whistle because it most of the time does completely upend your entire life. And so I think it is good to kind of compensate for that. But what is almost more important is that it has created an industry where there are lawyers who will work for free on contingency. If that whistleblower does collect a reward, the lawyer gets paid some amount of money. And so what's amazing is that there are people like me who have not collected a reward and may never collect a reward, but have benefited from free legal counsel from these types of lawyers. And so I think that is absolutely fantastic. So my number one piece of advice is if you are in a situation anywhere close to this one is to go talk to a lawyer, and it's very possible that you can talk to a lawyer for free, which I think is oftentimes the biggest barrier to talking to a lawyer. Yeah. And then to founders, I also recommend being very, very conscious and intentional about how you go about building a culture. Because like what we saw at Theranos was Elizabeth over 10 years built this culture of fear and at your startup you need to do the exact opposite. And yeah, and another thing that Theranos did really well was they took every opportunity to reinforce that culture of fear. And at other organizations, at your organization, whoever I'm speaking to, you need to take every opportunity to do the opposite of that. It's like whether the transgression is a big fraud or whether it's something very small, you need to take it seriously and you need to really test the internal tools that you have, like whether either from the company side or from the whistleblower side. Like a lot of companies do have internal whistleblowing tools that if you're an employee, I would recommend using and getting used to using before you see the giant fraud. Like start with the small stuff and take the small stuff really seriously. And I like to say, flex that ethical muscle and get used to doing the right thing when you see it. And I just think that so often in our lives we see some transgression and we don't do anything about it. And I think a good place to start is to just recognize when that's happening. Recognize, oh, that made me uncomfortable and I didn't do anything. What can I do next time? And then try to do that. And then you'll learn, you know, what types of resources are available to you. You'll learn who your support network is. And I also think it's a great way for companies to show that they're actually accountable to their core values. And first of all, I think a lot of startups don't even have defined core values. So that is like one piece of advice, is actually... and even the ACFE, the Association of Certified Fraud Examiners, have a lot of data and one of the most interesting things is that companies that just have defined core values pay less fines. Just by the act of defining core values and communicating that to your employees, you end up incurring fewer fines, which I think is just amazing. It's a relatively simple thing to do, to go through an exercise of defining core values. And then when you see these, I don't know what to call them, like instances where their core values are challenged, you use those as moments to show your employees that you're accountable to those core values, that they're really important to you and that they should be important to your employees. And I think that's how you go about building this culture. And if you're intentional about it for 10 years, I think you can end up with a really, really great culture where you're not just avoiding being a big fraud, but it's actually like a superpower because it allows you to see problems before they really grow into these huge, huge issues that can truly threaten the existence of your company. It's almost like it allows you to see around corners in some ways. It allows you to hear what your employees are thinking but not actually saying. I think that's such an important point because I think most founders don't set out to ever be unethical. I think what it is is it's this very slow chipping away because you can always rationalize doing something that you think is going to increase shareholder value or because your board nudged you or one of your big investors. And so it's like what you're saying, like if you just exercise this muscle a little bit all the time, then that slow chipping away gets counterbalanced. Exactly. Yeah. And if you have those core values, then it's something to hold yourself accountable to and you can see when you're starting to make decisions that don't align with those core values. And so it's at least like you're setting some kind of goalpost so you're at least cognizant of when that moral slippage starts to happen. Mike Milken: Who Gets Access to Capital? | Podcast | In Good Company

Norges Bank Investment Management(137.0K)·39:27·21.5K views·2026-11-11
Mike MilkenFinancier and philanthropist Nicolai TangenCEO, Norges Bank Investment Management
TL;DRMike Milken, pioneer of the high-yield bond market, discusses how a chance encounter at age 19 shaped his lifelong mission to expand access to capital, drive medical research, and foster upward mobility.
Joined by host Nicolai Tangen, they explore the realities of modern credit markets, human capital, and the global shifts in entrepreneurship. Genesis at Age 19: Catalyst Event: Mike Milken met a young African American man at the August 11, 1965 Watts Riots in Los Angeles who...

TL;DR

Mike Milken, pioneer of the high-yield bond market, discusses how a chance encounter at age 19 shaped his lifelong mission to expand access to capital, drive medical research, and foster upward mobility. Joined by host Nicolai Tangen, they explore the realities of modern credit markets, human capital, and the global shifts in entrepreneurship.

High-Yield Bonds and the Evolution of Credit Markets

  • Genesis at Age 19:
    • Catalyst Event: Mike Milken met a young African American man at the August 11, 1965 Watts Riots in Los Angeles who stated he lacked access to capital because of the color of his skin.
    • Research Discovery: Milken studied credit history at Berkeley and discovered that Great Depression data proved lower-quality debt yielded higher risk-adjusted returns, while sovereign debt carried the highest historical risk despite AAA ratings.
  • Overcoming Institutional Resistance:
    • Drexel's Transition: Milken utilized multiple-choice questions and comparative tests (showing buy-recommendation stocks had non-investment grade debt while sells had investment grade debt) to guide colleagues toward self-discovery.
    • The 1974 Turning Point: When the stock market dropped 50% and interest rates doubled, straight non-investment grade debt generated 100% returns between late 1974 and 1976, waking up skeptics.
  • Financing New Industries:
    • Pioneered Sectors: High-yield capital built the energy industry, biotech, cable, home building, hospitality/casinos (e.g., Mirage), and telecommunications.
    • MCI Telecom Impact: Long-distance phone calls cost $10/minute in the 1970s; financing MCI helped reduce costs by 75%, laying groundwork for modern digital communication.
    • Job Creation Impact: In the last 30 years of the 20th century, non-investment grade companies created over 60 million jobs in the US, compared to minus 4 million created by investment grade companies.

Human Capital, Wealth, and Global Shifts

  • Valuation of Human vs. Financial Assets:
    • Gary Becker's Concept: Milken applied Nobel laureate Gary Becker's human capital framework, noting that US human and social capital is valued at over $2,000 trillion, dwarfing financial assets at roughly $300 trillion and US GDP of around $30 trillion.
  • Global Wealth Migration:
    • Millionaire Destinations: In 2023–2024, the UAE became the top global destination for millionaires, followed by the US in second and Singapore in third.
    • Domestic Shifts: Entrepreneurs like Elon Musk (SpaceX moving to Texas) and Alex Karp (Palantir moving headquarters to Florida) highlight massive state-to-state and international shifts in job creation and capital.
  • The American Dream Center:
    • Washington DC Initiative: Opened in September near the White House, the center serves as a beacon of hope for the one-third of the population doubting upward mobility, anchored by four pillars: education, healthcare, financial literacy, and finding new entrepreneurs.

Private Credit, Systemic Risks, and Lessons from Drexel

  • Private Credit vs. Public Credit:
    • Nature of the Market: Milken views private credit as largely similar to public non-investment grade debt, functioning as a throwback to 1950s/1960s insurance company lending.
    • Fee Structure: Money managers charge 3 to 5 times more to manage money in private credit, creating strong internal incentives.
  • Maturity Mismatches:
    • Banking Failures: Silicon Valley Bank and Republic Bank failed not due to asset credit quality, but from financing intermediate US government securities and long-term assets with overnight/short-term liabilities during surging interest rates.
  • The Drexel Legacy:
    • Alumni Success: Former Drexel colleagues who went on to lead firms like Apollo (e.g., Tony Ressler) and Ares, or Goldman Sachs, retained a core belief in financing people and ideas first.
    • Changing Motivations: In the 1970s, 25% of finance entrants stated they wanted to "change the world"; today, incoming classes see that drop to roughly 1% due to massive compensation inflation.

Medical Research, Longevity, and Motivation

  • Economic Impact of Healthcare:
    • Life Expectancy Gains: Global average life expectancy rose from 31 years in 1900 to the mid-70s today through antibiotics and public health advancements.
    • Growth Driver: Milken notes that more than 50% of all global economic growth has come from advances in healthcare and public health.
  • Personal Drivers at Age 80:
    • Family Perspective: 97% of Milken's efforts are focused on the world his grandchildren will inherit, driven by his father's early lesson that everyone must feel they have a chance to succeed.

Mike Volpi on Why AI Infrastructure Will Outlast the Hype Cycle

a16z(309.0K)·56:06·12.0K views·2026-11-11
Mike VolpiInvestor Raghu RaghuramHost, a16z Guido AppenzellerHost, a16z
TL;DRThe AI infrastructure buildout shares structural parallels with the late-1990s internet boom, but the economic value of intelligence exceeds that of communication, and GPUs are proving to have a longer useful life than anticipated.
While an economic correction is likely for weaker neo-clouds, the underlying infrastructure assets will remain highly valuable over the long term. Historical Parallels: Mike Volpi compares today’s AI investment to the late '90s fiber-optic boom, noting that history "doesn't...

TL;DR

The AI infrastructure buildout shares structural parallels with the late-1990s internet boom, but the economic value of intelligence exceeds that of communication, and GPUs are proving to have a longer useful life than anticipated. While an economic correction is likely for weaker neo-clouds, the underlying infrastructure assets will remain highly valuable over the long term.

The AI Infrastructure Buildout vs. the 1990s Internet Boom

  • Historical Parallels: Mike Volpi compares today’s AI investment to the late '90s fiber-optic boom, noting that history "doesn't repeat itself, but it definitely rhymes." Both eras featured high expectations of transformative societal impact, parallel surges in demand, and massive influxes of debt and equity capital.
  • Technology & Demand Differences:
    • Bandwidth vs. Compute: Fiber capacity grew exponentially through WDM (adding colors of light to fiber), whereas compute is bound by a more moderated pace resembling Moore's Law.
    • Revenue Adoption: Model providers (Anthropic, OpenAI, open-source model providers like Fireworks, Together, and Model) are experiencing revenue ramps far superior to the internet bandwidth providers of the '90s.
    • Scale: The current AI buildout represents roughly 2% of global GDP, reflecting a larger macroeconomic investment because it builds "intelligence" rather than mere communication.
  • Asset Longevity & Scarcity:
    • GPU Useful Life: Despite expectations that Hopper (H100) chips would approach obsolescence after a few years, severe manufacturing scarcity has kept them fully utilized at high rental rates ($2.50 to $3.00/hour).
    • Token Efficiency: The ultimate metric is token revenue per watt. Newer architectures (Blackwell, Vera Rubin) convert electricity to dollars more efficiently, but extreme scarcity keeps older generations in demand.
    • Bankruptcies & Asset Reuse: Similar to how '90s fiber networks survived provider bankruptcies to serve future utility, today's compute assets will be fully utilized long-term, though ownership may shift if weaker neo-clouds collapse.

Enterprise M&A Playbook: Lessons from Cisco and Nvidia

  • Cisco’s M&A Strategy: During Volpi's tenure starting in 1994, Cisco completed roughly 70 acquisitions, which eventually accounted for 50% of the company's $30 billion in revenue.
    • Statistical Success: Cisco maintained a risk-tolerant model where roughly one-third of acquisitions succeeded wildly, one-third performed moderately, and one-third failed.
    • Valuation & Currency: Most deals were all-equity transactions capped at 4% to 5% of Cisco's market cap. Networking acquisitions integrated rapidly because they relied on open protocols (OSPF, BGP, Ethernet).
  • Nvidia's Strategic Position:
    • Aggressive Expansion: Nvidia has acquired foundational assets like Mellanox (the world's largest networking company) and taken equity/financing stakes in ~200 neo-clouds to shape industry structure and avoid disintermediation by a handful of hyperscalers.
    • The Limits of Industry Shaping: Volpi notes that industries dominated by scale economies (like the 3 major hyperscalers or airlines) naturally consolidate; funding weaker startups/neo-clouds is a noble effort for innovation, but ultimately futile against natural market consolidation.
  • Integration Best Practices: Guido Appenzeller and Mike Volpi agree that post-acquisition integration requires rigid, centralized repeatable processes rather than treating integration as an ad-hoc one-time event. Larger acquisitions (such as Cisco's purchase of Scientific Atlanta) suffer from severe cultural and operational friction.

Semiconductor Technology, Silicon, and Architecture

  • System-Level Architecture:
    • Monolithic vs. Patchwork: Modern AI workloads require treating the entire infrastructure as a single system. Google's TPU architecture represents the gold standard of system-level design, whereas mixing disparate chips (e.g., Cerebras with Arista switches) introduces architectural inefficiencies.
    • The Rack as a Unit: The minimum deployable unit has shifted from individual cards to entire racks (such as Nvidia's Blackwell configurations) and whole data-center rows (utilizing optical circuit switching).
  • Silicon Evolution & Memory Constraints:
    • Diversity in Chip Design: As model architectures diversify (FP4, FP8, mixture of experts, varying parameter counts), silicon diversity will increase through hardware-software co-design.
    • Reasoning Intensity: Reasoning-driven AI workloads require large parameter counts and massive KV caches, heavily favoring architectures with high memory bandwidth (such as 3D DRAM and hybrid copper bonding).
    • Packaging Challenges: Integrating logic and memory chips into the same package introduces severe thermal management hurdles due to differing heat profiles.

Networking: The Innovator's Dilemma in Data Centers

  • The Death of Statistical Multiplexing: Traditional data center networking (Broadcom Tomahawk/Dune architectures) relies on stochastic packet switching (stat-muxing variable-length IP packets), designed for unpredictable internet traffic patterns.
  • Predictable AI Workloads: AI training and batch-inference workloads feature continuous, highly predictable, fully utilized traffic flows.
  • The Need for Circuit Switching: Volpi argues that the future of data center networking will shift away from expensive, complex stat-muxed packet switches toward optical circuit switches, Time Division Multiplexing (TDM), or MEMS-based systems—posing a classic innovator's dilemma for legacy networking vendors.

Portfolio Strategy and Founder M&A Advice

  • Evaluating Exit Options: When advising startup boards on acquisition offers, founders must evaluate whether the leadership team has the "fire" to push through to the next phase, and whether the business has a realistic path to 10x growth rather than just doubling.
  • Continuous Intelligence Gathering: Volpi advises founders to reject passive inbound acquisition offers (which usually indicate an auction or a missed window) and instead maintain an ongoing dialogue with competitors and major players (Nvidia, Amazon, OpenAI) through regular coffee meetings as an "inverse intelligence gathering mechanism."

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Mike Volpi predicts there will be an economic correction and cleaning up of the industry structure affecting weaker neo-cloud balance sheets.
  • Mike Volpi predicts that fab capacity constraints will persist, meaning the semiconductor manufacturing bottleneck will not change significantly over the next 2 to 3 years, and potentially up to 5 years out.
  • Mike Volpi predicts that data center networking architectures will eventually abandon stochastic stat-muxed packet switching in favor of predictable circuit-switched or optical designs.

OpenAI’s chief economist on the jobs AI will actually create

Reid Hoffman(116.0K)·73:14·2026-11-11
Ronnie ChatterjiChief Economist, OpenAI Reid HoffmanHost, Possible Podcast Aria FingerHost, Possible Podcast
TL;DROpenAI’s first chief economist, Ronnie Chatterji, projects that AI productivity gains will become visible in national statistics within 1–2 years, driven largely by an accelerating "agentic revolution" across the enterprise.
He also warns against overestimating near-term growth rates or unemployment spikes, while arguing that a successful AI economy will ultimately expand the care and education sectors. Productivity Payoff Timeline: Ronnie Chatterji expects a much shorter lag than the 1990s...

TL;DR

OpenAI’s first chief economist, Ronnie Chatterji, projects that AI productivity gains will become visible in national statistics within 1–2 years, driven largely by an accelerating "agentic revolution" across the enterprise. He also warns against overestimating near-term growth rates or unemployment spikes, while arguing that a successful AI economy will ultimately expand the care and education sectors.

The Economic Impact: Timelines, Productivity, and Growth Realities

  • Productivity Payoff Timeline: Ronnie Chatterji expects a much shorter lag than the 1990s computer age, predicting broader payoff in productivity numbers within 1 to 2 years.
      • *GDP and Infrastructure:* AI is already showing up in GDP through the CAPEX channel (hardware investments contributing 40 basis points to 1% of GDP growth).
      • *Historical Comparison:* In 1987, Robert Solow observed computers everywhere except productivity statistics; the boom didn't arrive until the late 1990s.
  • Calibrating Growth Expectations: Ronnie Chatterji notes that even a 1 percentage point increase in US GDP growth would be transformational, and 2 percentage points would be distinctly felt.
      • *Discourse Check:* Expecting 10% growth in the next couple of years misjudges macroeconomic models.
  • Unemployment Outlook: Ronnie Chatterji considers a rapid 3X surge in unemployment (similar to the dramatic COVID-19 jump from the beginning of 2020 to March/April) to be an unlikely model for AI over the next 12 to 18 months.

Enterprise Adoption & Workplace Transformation

  • The Agentic Revolution: Ronnie Chatterji states that the world is in the early innings of agentic AI.
      • *Software Connectors:* Only a relatively small portion of firms have turned on connectors (linking AI to existing software applications), which is a major unlock.
      • *Technical vs. Non-Technical Lag:* At OpenAI, research and engineering teams reached 100% agentic workflows by late 2025, while non-technical functions (like economics) fully agentified by April or May of 2026—a 4 to 5 month lag.
  • Internal Job Evolution: Ronnie Chatterji highlights that while headline unemployment hasn't shifted drastically, the underlying work has: professionals are increasingly stepping outside traditional job descriptions (e.g., finance professionals doing marketing or people ops; communications staff running numbers).
  • Early Adopter Sectors: Ronnie Chatterji advises that legal and HR/people operations are two organizational functions adopting AI rapidly due to their structured data and processes.
  • Global Enterprise Dispersion:
      • *East Japan and South Korea:* Show heavy enterprise adoption and optimism, driven by past tech success, favorable demographics, and chip supply chain integration (e.g., SK Hynix, Samsung).
      • *Europe:* Faces active internal debates over enterprise productivity, stack sovereignty, and shifting geopolitics (as evidenced by the Draghi memo).
      • *Demographics:* Users aged 18–25 drive heavy daily use globally. Countries with young populations like Indonesia and Brazil show zero lag in student adoption compared to San Francisco.

Entrepreneurship and the Solopreneur Boom

  • Lowering Startup Friction: Ronnie Chatterji notes AI acts as an accelerant for new company formation, helping entrepreneurs brainstorm, iterate, and navigate ideation friction (historically the most common failure point).
  • Failure as Learning: Ronnie Chatterji and Reid Hoffman emphasize that quicker experimentation enabled by AI leads to faster failures, which provide vital lessons for future foundings or co-founding journeys.
  • Global and Small Business Reach:
      • *Venture Capital Access:* In regions with lower VC access, founders use AI to bridge gaps (such as borrowing an expert AI chief marketing officer).
      • *Usage Stats:* OpenAI released a report 6 months prior showing roughly 4 million Americans use ChatGPT in the entrepreneurial process.
      • *Sole Proprietors:* Small-scale operators (like a sole piano teacher) can optimize marketing, billing, and customer acquisition.

The Future Care Economy and Bureaucracy

  • Expanding Eds and Meds: Ronnie Chatterji argues that in a successful 2031 AI-driven economy, societal wealth will naturally flow into the care economy—expanding employment in education and healthcare (currently 1 in 7 US jobs) rather than shrinking it, mirroring historical trends where tech adoption adds human headcount alongside efficiency.
  • Funding the Care Bounty: Ronnie Chatterji suggests that productivity gains and potential shifts in taxing labor and capital can generate an "AI dividend" to fund these sectors and improve service quality.
  • Navigating Bureaucracy: Rather than just providing static information (like old internet FAFSA forms), Ronnie Chatterji envisions persistent agents acting as "navigators" to help low-income students, patients, and citizens navigate complex bureaucratic and allocation systems.

Geopolitics, Trade, and National Security

  • Geoeconomics Shift: Ronnie Chatterji points out a sea change in public policy where traditional economics has merged with national security (geoeconomics), requiring deep tech policies around semiconductors, quantum computing, energy, and AI models.
  • Trade-Offs in Tech Export: Ronnie Chatterji notes that unlike the consumer internet era (which maximized total addressable markets by population), deep tech models involve national security externalities (e.g., cyber capabilities), meaning unrestricted export may be heavily restricted.

OpenAI Inside Perspective & Economic Research

  • Role of a Frontier Lab Economist: Ronnie Chatterji explains that his team's core duties are interpreting vast internal data safely for society and policymakers, and applying economic theory to the emerging "agent economy" (where MBAs will manage more agents than people).
  • Bridging the Academic Gap: To combat credibility deficits, Chatterji's team publishes peer-reviewed working papers, shares data with external academics, and runs programs like Exchange (funding 14 different external research teams to study OpenAI data independently).
  • GDP Val Benchmark: Developed by OpenAI’s Frontier Evals team, GDP Val tests AI against real-world tasks written and judged by experienced practitioners across 44 occupations to measure practical work impact rather than abstract test scores.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Ronnie Chatterji predicts that the bigger payoff of AI will reach national productivity statistics within the next 1 to 2 years (12 to 18 months).
  • Ronnie Chatterji predicts that the agentic revolution will permeate the rest of enterprise software and the global economy over the next 12 to 18 months.
  • Ronnie Chatterji predicts that by 2030 (the end of 2030), US unemployment will be 5% or lower.
  • Ronnie Chatterji predicts that the share of American workers using AI daily will reach up to 80% by 2030 (up from roughly 40% currently measured by HBS surveys).
  • Ronnie Chatterji predicts that by 2036, the economy will feature fully functioning agentic organizations capable of operating at scale.

Notable Quotes

"The future is already here. It's just unevenly distributed." — Ronnie Chatterji (paraphrasing William Gibson)
"When I joined OpenAI, AI couldn't do my my daughter's math homework. And now we're solving problems that haven't been solved by humans in 100 years. ... That is in two years. And that is that you have to sit with that and be in awe." — Ronnie Chatterji

That's called the O*NET, right? So O*NET is basically a list of tasks. And then you ask, okay, can AI do this thing? And machine learning people love benchmarks, right? So you're able then to test the AI against these tasks and say, does it do it better than a human? Is it equal? What do you do with ties? All that kind of stuff. Originally, though, I think a of people looked at, let's say, 10 tasks in Ronnie Chatterji's job, and they said, if AI can do five of them better than Ronnie, he's got like a 50 % chance of being unemployed. That's not how it works at all. Like I I just can't be more emphatic that that is not how it works at all. I still remember when my dad got a computer in his office in 1984. He ran big regressions and stuff. He'd go into like a big mainframe computer room with a punch card. He'd tell me all these stories. He got the computer. Every single one of his tasks as an economics professor, I was second generation one, he basically could be done by the computer. But he didn't he wasn't out of work in 1984. He used the computer to run bigger regressions, more models, more work, write more papers. And so the idea that exposure to AI equals bad is actually not the case at all. In fact, sometimes exposure can be good if you can leverage AI for what you're doing. The other thing people forget is, you know, tasks are for a lot of us endogenous to the job. It's not like someone just says, Ronnie, you're only going to do these 10 things. Ronnie, you have a high-level objective that's a little bit fuzzy. Here are the 10 things that most people do, and you figure out the things you want to do. And then you realize, oh gosh, I can reach that objective in other ways. I'll add task 11 and 12 and I'll subtract one and two. That's how a lot of us, particularly in white-collar work, work. So I think while it was really interesting to look at the task-based view and kind of machine learning benchmarks as a result of that, it led to a misleading assumption about how many jobs would be destroyed by AI. What Comes After Loops?: Deep Dive Into Agentic Architecture | DevDay 2026

OpenAI(2.1M)·24:01·32.2K views·2026-11-11
Raj BhatekEngineer, OpenAI EricEngineer, OpenAI
TL;DROpenAI engineers Raj Bhatek and Eric traced the evolution of AI agents from simple prompt-response loops to collaborative, multi-agent systems—culminating in a massive 10,000-agent architecture that successfully tackled the Navier-Stokes Millennium Prize problem.
Initial Paradigm Shift: - Early AI interactions required manual human intervention (e.g., copying and pasting code like FizzBuzz). - The introduction of reasoning combined with tool use (such as in the o3 launch) created the foundational "agentic loop." - The Codex General...
  • TL;DR: OpenAI engineers Raj Bhatek and Eric traced the evolution of AI agents from simple prompt-response loops to collaborative, multi-agent systems—culminating in a massive 10,000-agent architecture that successfully tackled the Navier-Stokes Millennium Prize problem.

Evolution of Agentic Architecture: From Loops to Codex

  • Initial Paradigm Shift:
    • Early AI interactions required manual human intervention (e.g., copying and pasting code like FizzBuzz).
    • The introduction of reasoning combined with tool use (such as in the o3 launch) created the foundational "agentic loop."
  • The Codex General Agent:
    • Built as a union of a model and a harness working together; OpenAI open-sourced Codex with a minimal, opinionated design giving models access to a computer via shell, file system, and computer use.
    • The Responses API manages the tool-call loop, execution sandbox, and conversation context.
  • Solving Long-Horizon Work via Compaction:
    • Early models hit context window limits and stopped prematurely.
    • OpenAI trained native compaction into both the harness and model (accessible via the `/compact` endpoint in the Responses API), allowing models to resume from a condensed state and handle multi-window tasks.
  • From Outer Loops to Native Persistence:
    • Developers initially used manual scripts or brute-force loops (like Geoffrey Huntley's "Ralph loop" or using `continue` statements) to keep models working.
    • Introduced `--goal` in GPT-5.5 to track objectives outside the core context window.
    • With GPT-5.6, persistence was trained directly into the model, eliminating the need for `--goal` hacks. Wharton professor Ethan Mullica notably tested this by having Codex play *Slay the Spire 2* for five hours straight.

Multi-Agent Workflows & Best Practices

  • Scaling from Single Agents:
    • Eric (DX team) advises starting with a single agent and clear verification methods for tasks before scaling up to teams.
  • Autonomous Coordination (GPT-6):
    • GPT-6 series models can coordinate and collaborate natively, breaking down massive tasks (like full codebase refactors or game ports) by spinning up subagents (e.g., Ultra) autonomously.
  • Agent Geometries and Effort Scaling:
    • Effort Scaling: Dynamically assigns reasoning effort and models using subagents (e.g., Sol managing integration, Astra handling difficult test failures, and Luna taking on simple busy work).
    • The Pair Pattern: Deploying a pair of subagents with explicit communication boundaries (e.g., one agent handling capture tech in an old game engine, the other unpacking/visualizing the scene in Blender).
    • Thread Managers: Using ChatGPT app threads to spin up and manage separate conversations for discrete tasks (like a separate chat for every PR in a review queue) while keeping the manager informed.
    • Departmental Roles: Structuring long-running projects (like video games) by assigning agents distinct lanes (art director, playtester, programmer, producer).
  • Pitfalls to Avoid:
    • Middle Management Tax: Overcomplicating agent hierarchies with too many rules causes agents to stall seeking approval.
    • Context Drift & The Work Log: To counteract short-term memory loss and constant human nagging/steering, prompt Codex to maintain a compact work log containing the overall goal, running work, and how recent steering relates to both.

Scaling to the Next Generation: Agent Teams

  • Massive-Scale Collaboration:
    • Moving beyond a handful of agents to thousands (e.g., 10,000 agents) to tackle massive, complex problems.
  • The Navier-Stokes Breakthrough:
    • OpenAI utilized an internal advanced model alongside Codex to solve the long-standing Millennium Prize Navier-Stokes problem using 10,000 agents, generating 2.7 million messages and 130 billion output tokens.
  • Agent Team Architecture:
    • Unified Goal with Diversity: Initialized multiple teams with different versions of a problem to encourage varied approaches and prevent duplicative effort.
    • Cross-Team Communication: Agents share findings and ask questions across different teams.
    • Dynamic Orchestration: As hypotheses were explored and new questions arose, teams were dynamically created, steered, or redirected toward promising lines (e.g., all teams being diverted to the unforced Euler result once found).
    • Broader Impact: Using this internal model architecture, OpenAI resolved more than 100 longstanding problems across mathematics and continues making discoveries in math and science.

Notable Quotes

"As models get better, things change. How we build them really does, too." — Raj Bhatek
"Every agent Codex brings in can dramatically increase token use without necessarily making the work faster or better. It's up to you to decide when a problem needs the extra help." — Eric
"Try not to overthink managing all your agents. It's just often not worth the hassle, and it makes it very easy to lose track of how complex your agent teams have gotten." — Eric
Hey, everybody. How's it going? Great. My name is Raj Bhatek. I'm part of our Applied AI engineering team here at OpenAI, and excited to be with you all today. When we were naming the session, What Comes After Loops, we joked that by the time we finished the content, everything would be outdated. So what we have today, it's for today. But that's honestly what building agents has been like for the past year or so. As models get better, things change. How we build them really does, too. So today, Eric and I are going to rewind a bit, tell you about how we got here, some of the design decisions we made here at OpenAI, what you can do with agents today, and then share some stuff that we've been cooking. So before we get started, I want to rewind a bit. I want to take you guys back to when we launched ChatGPT. So when it first came out, the first thing I did was I had ChatGPT write FizzBuzz. Show of hands, how many of you know what FizzBuzz is? Cool. OK. For those of you who don't, FizzBuzz is a very common coding interview question we give to junior developers. ChatGPT gave me the code. I copied and pasted it into my code editor, and I ran it. What was really, really cool was it ran. It actually worked. And everybody in the back can hear me, right? Just want to make sure? Cool. Sweet. So I was very blown away. I'm assuming a lot of you had a similar reaction as well. AI had gotten really, really smart, and it was the beginning of something big. But what was different was I was still the one copying and pasting things, right? I would write something in ChatGPT, copy to my IDE. I hadn't seen what happened. But you know, that changes when we let AI take action. This video may look familiar. This is really when we let AI combine two things, reasoning and tool use. The video may look, this is from the 03 launch video. We gave 03 the task to analyze an image. You can see how it works with the image. It can zoom in. It can reason over the details. It can inspect things, figure out what to do next. That was really the big shift from the previous example, where the AI was actually now using tools. It could take action. It could reason over the result. And it could plan its next step. This is what we call the agentic loop. In this example, we give it very bespoke tools. But what happens when we give it access to a computer? This is when we introduced Codex. Codex is our general agent here at OpenAI. And our design philosophy here is we want the agent to be able to act on its reasoning. It could try and approach. It could see what happened. It could change course. And really, we built the Codex around this idea of a model and a harness working together. What's also important is we train the model to actually work within this harness. So it understood the tools it had. It could learn how to use them. It could understand the feedback it was getting. And this union of the model and the harness is really what formed the Codex agent. And really, how we design the harness informs how we do training and vice versa. How we train the model informs how we build the harness. So I want to dig into that a bit. Let's think back to our FizzBuzz example. I had to copy and paste code. Before, both Codex and then a big jump we saw with GPT-5, we now just hand over the whole task to the model. Codex could write the code. It could run it. It could check the output. If it made a mistake, it would then just go and fix it. What was cool was we could even automate FizzBuzz. So I now want to go and take a look under the hood of what the harness actually looks like. So really think about the harness. Think of it as managing the instructions, the tools, and what the model actually sees, and the actual execution environment of its actions itself. It also manages the context, so what it carries from the next step to the next. And with Codex, we were very opinionated. You'll notice Codex is open source, by the way. We kept it very minimal. We want to give it very generalizable tools. And we want to think, hey, if we give it access to a computer, what does it need to act? So from the shell to the way it uses the file system, or how it uses computer use, we were very opinionated about how we made the design. So the way you should think about Codex is, hey, if I can do a task on the computer, I want to give the model the ability to figure it out. We want the model to generalize, and just like you and I would be using the computer. So how does it work? Really, at the center of everything is the Responses API. It's a loop that we manage in the Responses API. We pass the tool definitions to the model. And now when the model requests a tool call, we manage the execution within the sandbox itself. We then manage the actual conversation context, and we append that. And the harness really decides whether we take a next step or a next turn. I talked about this a bit earlier, but the harness changed the way we approach training. And what we learned from building the harness informs how we train the model, and the capabilities we built into the model inform how we build the harness. I think the best example of this is how we trained compaction into both the harness and the model, especially in situations where one context window wasn't enough. For those of you who are familiar with this idea of context, really think of it as the information the model has in front of it. This could be its instructions, the history of the work, the task. And as the agent kind of executes tool calls, as it reads files, as it checks results, the history grows. But that context window still has a limit. For long tasks, we need to make sure that we're giving it room to keep working but not forget about the work it's already done. And that's where your native compaction was really, really big in Codex. As the context window filled up, sorry, and everybody can hear me, right? Sorry, I just want to make sure. Louder. Louder. OK, yes, I will talk louder. I promise. OK. As the window fills up, we request compaction through the responses API. So if you guys have seen the slash compact endpoint, the responses API, we're calling that under the hood. This condenses the history of the conversation. It works into a stellar state, carries the bill forward. But what's really special in Codex itself is we actually train this idea of compaction into the model. So the model understands what it means to be compacted. It understands what it means to work forward from a compacted state. It understands to resume from compaction. So it understands what to keep in the conversation and what to remove as it goes forward. This unlocked a really big thing for us, and this was long horizon work. So the model could work across multiple context windows, it could work across multiple compaction events, and now that it had the capacity to work longer, the next task was how do we actually get it to keep going until it was done. Earlier models didn't understand the idea of what done meant, and they would make good progress, but they would just end their turn early. I know I was not too happy about this, and this is something that Peter Steinberger, the creator of OpenClaw, put on Twitter. It was just a way of getting the model to work longer and longer, and it was purely to cue a bunch of continue statements over and over again. What Peter and pretty much all of us were doing was creating this idea of an outer loop, right? You just keep nudging the model until it finishes. So at this point we really needed to understand how do you get the model to keep working? And this was really the foundational idea behind loop engineering. Geoffrey Huntley made this very popular with the idea of a Ralph loop. How many of you know what a Ralph loop is? Cool, sweet. So this was a kind of a brute force idea of like, hey, let me give it a bash loop, let me get the agent to work against that prompt file, and have it work over and over again. And we'd start each run with a fresh context window, we'd pick up the files from the last run, and we'd keep going. What was really cool about the loop is it kept the agents just working, right? They would work towards some sort of objective, they would check what was done, they would keep going. It was really for us to manage how that loop was executed, right? How do I know if I made progress? How do I know when to stop? But really this philosophy of like, keep iterating, and like trying again and again was this idea of loop engineering. We brought that pattern into Codex with dash goal. How many of you used dash goal before? Cool, sweet. So we did this with GPT 5.5, and the idea here was we wanted to make sure it survived beyond the end of a turn, right? With goal, we gave Codex an objective, the harness saves it separately outside of the actual core context window so it can't be compacted, it tracks the status, and then it sees if the agent is done working. What was cool about dash goal was since it was saved outside of the actual conversation window, you could just work for a very long time. You could work across multiple compaction events, and because there was always that nudge or that check to keep working, the model knew like, hey, I have to keep going. Or if there was an actual genuine blocker, it could make a request and say, hey, I'm blocked, I actually need to stop. And since this was done independently, the goal could survive the end of a turn, and it would just keep working until it got the job done. But this gave us a really good kind of signal for training. Hey, we understand what persistence is, why don't we just build that into the model? And with GPT 5.6, that was our really big unlock. We trained persistence into the model itself. The model could understand what useful steps meant, they could check its own work, and it wouldn't need another set of continues. Toby Luque, the CEO of Shopify, described it as the first model that no longer needed Dashgoal, it just kept going until it got the job done. And Ethan Mullica, Wharton professor of AI, said he gave Codex access to his computer and had it placed Slay the Spire 2 for about five hours straight. We now have models that could reason, they could generalize new tasks, they could use a computer like you and I, and they could work coherently over a very long horizon. But I introduced a new set of challenges, right? Like, how do we get the model to work faster? How do we get to parallelize work? And we introduced this idea of multi-agents early into 5.6.0, but it wasn't until we got GPT 6, it was when we got this huge leap in intelligence, where we let the model actually coordinate itself. It understood how to decide to split up problems, work on different things. And now I'm going to hand it over to Eric to go into a bit more detail on how you can work from single agents to multiple agents. So, pass out to him. Hey, everyone. It's a much bigger crowd than I was expecting today. So I'm Eric. You may recognize me from my post on X. I work on the DX team at OpenAI. My role here is to serve as your bridge to the talented researchers and engineers working on Codex. And a big part of that is finding best practices and putting them into words for you all to do more with Codex. So, to build on Raj's walk down memory lane, let's take a look at Codex today and explore what's possible with all the capabilities we now have access to. When you're taking a long task, it's super tempting to start by building a whole team of agents. But you're almost always better off starting with just one and giving it a clear task and a way to check its work. The GPT-6 series can keep working, compact their context, and push through failures with no external loop telling them to continue. For many tasks, that's honestly enough. Only add more agents when the work gives you a reason to. Okay, so what about tasks where that won't work, like a complete code-based refactor or a whole game? With GPT-6, our models were trained to coordinate and collaborate from the jump. And they can tackle these hard problems by breaking them down into independent pieces and multitasking. Using Ultra, Codex will make copies of itself all on its own and collaborate with those copies to tackle even the largest of projects. But here's the thing. Every agent Codex brings in can dramatically increase token use without necessarily making the work faster or better. It's up to you to decide when a problem needs the extra help. Some tasks are obviously decomposable, like asking your agent to review five PRs. This makes it easy to be explicit, as you can ask Codex to assign a subagent to each one. Other tasks require broad research, like fixing a tricky race condition. Here, you can just ask Codex to use a couple of subagents to split up the investigation. A small nudge is enough for Codex to bring in help, and each added agent can keep its own working context and make progress on its part. With the right prompt, you can shape your agents into complex geometries. Be careful, though, as it's easy to build structures that don't add any value. So what if we could automatically manage the reasoning effort and the models assigned to a given task using subagents so we don't have to think about it? This is where effort scaling comes in. We can introduce a lever that allows Codex to ramp work up when it really matters and down when it doesn't. With the right skill, you can have Sol ask Astra for help when it is to spend a few turns iterating on a test failure and focus on the difficult part while keeping Sol in charge of the integration. All the while, Luna agents can be handed simpler supporting tasks that don't need as much attention from Sol. This won't always cost you less, as even Astra can't perfectly predict which model will solve the task at the best price, but in many cases, it can be a huge help in helping you reduce the decision anxiety in picking the right model for the job. So here's a shortened version of a skill I've been working on called Coordinate. You can take a picture of it if you're around there. It's designed to be used by Sol Medium, which handles most work. When it runs into a problem it's having trouble with, it's told to ask for help from Astra High. When it encounters simple busy work, it can toss those problems to Luna High agents without a second thought. When I was working on a game porting project one weekend, I ran into a problem that got me to try a new agent structure. I'm calling it the pair. I had this old game I made years ago that was built with an ancient game engine I could no longer find. I had the project files and the running game though. I wanted to take this old game, run it, and then visually recreate the running scene in Blender so I could continue working on it in a new game engine. To do this, I had Codex summon a pair of agents where one side was tasked with getting the capture tech working in the old engine and the other agent was tasked with unpacking and visualizing the scene in a workable format in Blender. Here, the format could inform the capture tech so it made sense for the two agents to split the work and keep the communication flowing so that if one side found an optimization, the other could adapt. Setting up a pair takes a bit of special wording since you'll have to make sure to define the communication boundaries pretty explicitly. So this prompt is pretty technical. Take a picture of it and ask Codex what it thinks. Maybe it'll find the perfect task in your project for a pair of subagents. As you work in Codex, it's very common to have many different tasks running at the same time. Jumping between a bunch of threads can be pretty overwhelming though. An easy way to make this more manageable is to just ask Codex to manage the threads for you. In the ChatGPT app, Codex can start new threads and even send messages to and steer other running chats. These are distinct from subagents, with each chat which each chat can also use. Think of it as your thread manager, taking in your overall goal and replacing that loose script you used to set up manually. Setting this up is super easy. You can just ask Codex to do it. Here's an example where you can just have it spin up a separate chat for every PR in your review queue. What's great is that you can just open the chats after they've been started and steer them manually if you want to stay involved. The best part though is that because our agents are so naturally chatty, they'll also keep the manager involved with all your meddling. So the work keeps moving even if you decide you no longer want to see it through. One of the big benefits of Codex Compaction is that context can accumulate over time and a thread can start to specialize around the long-running task. Game projects can take months to complete and are naturally broken down into distinct work streams like art, design, programming, and play testing. Here you can assign each of those lanes an agent who can run them like a department lead. The art director can handle all the environment art while your play tester tries the latest build your programmer just finished. You can just give your notes and directions to the producer while your agent team handles the rest. When you're getting your game project started, try setting it up with a prompt like this. It may not be the most efficient way to work token-wise, but it's really handy to be able to drill down and brainstorm a new mechanic while your producer coordinates the next build to share. While agents love to collaborate, every time you make a new rule about how they're supposed to work together, they can start to stall out as they seek approval through made-up hierarchies. I call this the middle management tax. Try not to overthink managing all your agents. It's just often not worth the hassle, and it makes it very easy to lose track of how complex your agent teams have gotten. Now, before I wrap up today, I want to bring in one more thing that's made a huge difference in how well my agents have stayed on track through my constant steering. I don't know about you, but when I watch my agents work, I constantly nag them with little things I forgot to mention when I sent the first prompt. The problem is that after the agent compacts like three or four times, it might now have drifted from your original prompt, which still has a ton of important context about the overall goal. Agents only have so much short-term memory, and just like you, that little thing that you just can't get to just yet, that keeps nagging you, it'll just keep nagging you unless you write it down. The trick to keeping your models on track as you constantly nag them with steers is to just ask them to keep a compact work log that contains the overall goal, your running work, and how your last year relates to both. By writing it down, the agent can avoid losing track of active work streams or some weird precision you made to your agent when testing your beta version last Thursday. By keeping this work lag compact, though, it stops your agent from maintaining a never-ending diary of every test that ever failed and why. Ask your Codex to set one up and enjoy the freedom of just naturally micromanaging your agent without it getting completely lost as it works. Oh, and by the way, before I hand you back to Raj, I want you all to keep in mind that best practices have a shelf life and you should never get attached to your workflows.
