Instead of a handful of agents, how do we start thinking about things at really big scale? So what happens if we go from a few to a thousand? How do we get them to work cohesively? How do we get them to maintain coherence? And how do we make sure that they're working towards a singular goal? You may have heard about our work with the Navier-Stokes problem, which is a long-standing Millennium Prize problem. Here we used an internal model and Codex, and we found a solution with 10,000 agents working together. Across the full effort, there were 2.7 million messages and about 130 billion output tokens. I want everybody to take a minute to just take that in, right? 2.7 million messages and 10,000 agents working together. So when you think about that scale, how you divide the work matters, how you manage context, how do you make sure the agents collaborate, and how do you make sure that you divide the work correctly? All that changes at this scale. Now I want to go into a bit of the architecture of how we approach the problem. You can refer to this pattern as agent teams. We let groups of agent teams work towards a unified goal. Our objective is to do two things. We want to encourage both deep work, but we also want to encourage a wide research objective. Right? Let's walk through how we approached it. We started by initializing multiple teams with different versions of the problem and prompted it to encourage different approaches. So the way to really think about this is these are individual teams with multiple sub-agents underneath, and they're all working towards that unified goal. What's important here is communication, right? Not only can they communicate with themselves, but they can also communicate across different teams, right? We want to make sure that they can share ideas. If I have a finding, hey, cool, team one, you should know what team two just found out. I can ask questions of other teams. I can know what the other team is working on. And the big objective there is we want to prevent duplicative effort. This part's important, right? We talked about 10,000 agents. If all 10,000 are working on the same thing, that's really expensive, right? We want them to make sure that we're encouraging diversity, and we want to know what each team is working on, so they're not all trying the same thing over and over again. What was important is as the teams evolved, as they got deeper into their respective idea, what they actually did changed as well, right? One may have explored a hypothesis, one may have challenged assumptions, others ran experiments, but what was important is that every team still owned their own unique approach. As we got deeper into the problem, as new findings exposed new questions, we created and orchestrated new teams, right? We may have steered teams towards a more promising line, but the goal remained the same, right? The way we divided work can change, but the goal and objective does not. In Navier Stokes, when we found the unforced Euler result, we diverted all the teams towards solving the problem, and by the end of it, we had solved the solution. So, whew! This pattern has shown some incredible results for us, right? Especially with models that are even more powerful than GPT-6. With the internal model that we used to solve Navier Stokes, we were able to resolve more than 100 longstanding problems across mathematics, and we continue to use it to find new discoveries in both math and science. You’re probably sleepwalking through life (here's how to wake up) - Robert Greene

Smartphone Addiction: - Ubiquity: 90%+ of American adults have a smartphone, and an estimated 90%+ of those users are addicted to them, spending roughly 7 hours a day on the device. - Erosion of Agency: Algorithms on Instagram, X, and TikTok program users' thoughts,...

TL;DR

Author Robert Greene joins hosts Shaan Puri and Sam Parr on Episode 881 of My First Million to discuss his new book *The Law of the Sublime*, the crisis of modern smartphone addiction, and the necessity of stepping outside to reconnect with agency, awe, and human nature.

The Modern Malaise: Technology, Addiction, and Disconnection

  • Smartphone Addiction:
    • Ubiquity: 90%+ of American adults have a smartphone, and an estimated 90%+ of those users are addicted to them, spending roughly 7 hours a day on the device.
    • Erosion of Agency: Algorithms on Instagram, X, and TikTok program users' thoughts, feelings, and experiences, driving a deep, modern sense of powerlessness.
  • Situational Blindness:
    • Externalized Focus: People walk through beautiful natural settings like Los Angeles parks staring at their phones, which Greene characterizes as a form of mental illness.
    • Atrophied Brains: Easy, instantaneous gratification is rewiring the human brain, breeding a generation that lacks the patience to read a 400-page book or write an essay.

The Path to Reconnection: The Sublime and Inner Agency

  • Breaking the Cycle:
    • Cold Turkey Withdrawal: Leave the phone behind, lock it in a drawer, and go outside into nature where you are forced to interact with the non-human world.
    • Awe and Scale: Activities like climbing a mountain break people out of the tiny circle imposed by technology and restore a sense of wonder.
  • The Limits of External Stimulation:
    • The Flatness Trap: Constantly chasing external distractions leaves individuals feeling empty because the restless human mind immediately demands more.
    • Internal Fulfillment: True, lasting fulfillment comes from within—through physical adventure, love, or connecting with animals—experiences that cannot be programmed by a phone.

Historical Resilience and the Power of Purpose

  • The Andes Plane Crash (1971):
    • Extreme Survival: Uruguayan rugby players trapped in the 14,000-foot Andes mountains with no winter gear or climbing equipment survived by drawing on deep internal resources.
    • The Power of Will: Nando Parado climbed a vertical wall of ice and rock with improvised tools for two hours out of love and a sense of purpose to return to his father.
  • Viktor Frankl & Concentration Camp Survival:
    • Meaning as Survival: In Auschwitz, survivors were overwhelmingly those who had a distinct purpose, such as a book to write or a family member to save.

Mastering Power, Mystery, and Charisma

  • Core Lessons from *The 48 Laws of Power*:
    • Say Less Than Necessary: In the social media era, people reveal too much; power requires a balance of absence and presence.
    • Disappear: Celebrities like Beyoncé disappear for a year and a half before dropping a record, letting speculation build an air of mystery.
  • The Psychology of Charisma:
    • Childhood Trauma Origin: Many charismatic figures (e.g., Marilyn Monroe, Malcolm X) come from deeply troubled or abandoned backgrounds, making them outerdirected and desperate for public love.
    • Confidence as a Core Trait: True charisma stems from inner security and an intense drive behind a cause, whereas fake confidence reads as empty bravado.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • AI and Legal Professions: AI will "decimate" the legal profession because individuals will be able to get answers to any legal problem instantly, as stated by Robert Greene.
  • AI and Negative Human Nature: The dark side of human nature (irrationality, grandiosity, phishing, scams, and the creation of biological weapons/dirty bombs) will increasingly hijack AI technology over the coming years, according to Robert Greene.
  • AI and Public Resentment: Deep wells of public resentment and fear regarding AI will eventually force political movements to demand strict laws capping data centers and regulating the technology, predicted by Robert Greene.

Notable Quotes

"People are suffering from the narrowness of their experience. A lot of it coming from technology. There's something incredible about just simply being alive." — Robert Greene
"There's no experience that could be programmed on your stupid phone that can ever give you that kind of sensation... I did something difficult, arduous, but I feel wow look at this." — Robert Greene
"Power is about absence and presence. If you're always present, people take you for granted... grow bored with you. If you're always absent, you have there's nothing to talk about." — Robert Greene
"I just make what's cool to me and sometimes the whole world agrees." — Mike Posner (cited by Shaan Puri)
